### Schools

The Montana Territory was formed on April 26, 1864, when the U.S. passed the [Organic Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_Act).[^cite_note-269] Schools started forming in the area before it was officially a territory as families started settling into the area. The first schools were subscription schools that typically met in the teacher's home. The first formal school on record was at Fort Owen in Bitterroot valley in 1862. The students were Indian children and the children of Fort Owen employees. The first school term started in early winter and lasted only until February 28. Classes were taught by Mr. Robinson.[^cite_note-270] Another early subscription school was started by Thomas Dimsdale in Virginia City in 1863. In this school students were charged \$1.75 per week.[^cite_note-Sievert-271] The Montana Territorial Legislative Assembly had its inaugural meeting in 1864.[^cite_note-272] The first legislature authorized counties to levy taxes for schools, which set the foundations for public schooling.[^cite_note-273] Madison County was the first to take advantage of the newly authorized taxes and it formed the first public school in Virginia City in 1886.[^cite_note-Sievert-271] The first school year was scheduled to begin in January 1866, but severe weather postponed its opening until March. The first school year ran through the summer and did not end until August 17. One of the first teachers at the school was Sarah Raymond. She was a 25-year-old woman who had traveled to Virginia City via wagon train in 1865. To become a certified teacher, Raymond took a test in her home and paid a \$6 fee in gold dust to obtain a teaching certificate. With the help of an assistant teacher, Mrs. Farley,[^cite_note-Briggeman-274] Raymond was responsible for teaching 50 to 60 students each day out of the 81 students enrolled at the school. Sarah Raymond was paid \$125 per month, and Mrs. Farley was paid \$75 per month. No textbooks were used in the school. In their place was an assortment of books brought by various emigrants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Brien200314–16-275] Raymond quit teaching the following year, but she later became the Madison County superintendent of schools.[^cite_note-Briggeman-274]



[First Interstate Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Interstate_Center), in downtown [Billings](123654.md), is the tallest building in Montana.

## Culture

See also: [Music of Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Montana), [Artists from Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_Montana#Artists), and [Authors from Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_Montana)

Many well-known artists, photographers and authors have documented the land, culture and people of Montana in the last 130 years. Painter and sculptor [Charles Marion Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Marion_Russell), known as "the cowboy artist", created more than 2,000 paintings of [cowboys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboy), Native Americans, and landscapes set in the [Western United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_West) and in [Alberta](717.md), Canada.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Toronto2000-276] The [C. M. Russell Museum Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._M._Russell_Museum_Complex) in [Great Falls, Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Falls,_Montana), houses more than 2,000 Russell artworks, personal objects, and artifacts.

Pioneering feminist author, film-maker, and media personality [Mary MacLane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_MacLane) attained international fame in 1902 with her memoir of three months in her life in [Butte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butte,_Montana), *The Story of Mary MacLane*. She referred to Butte throughout the rest of her career and remains a controversial figure there for her mixture of criticism and love for Butte and its people.

[Evelyn Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evelyn_Cameron), a naturalist and photographer from [Terry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry,_Montana) documented early 20th-century life on the Montana prairie, taking startlingly clear pictures of everything around her: cowboys, sheepherders, weddings, river crossings, freight wagons, people working, badlands, eagles, coyotes and wolves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Travel2010-277]

Many notable Montana authors have documented or been inspired by life in Montana in both fiction and non-fiction works. [Pulitzer Prize](24230.md) winner [Wallace Earle Stegner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallace_Earle_Stegner) from Great Falls was often called "The Dean of Western Writers".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoswell2006-278] [James Willard Schultz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Willard_Schultz) ("Apikuni") from [Browning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Browning,_Montana) is most noted for his prolific stories about Blackfeet life and his contributions to the naming of prominent features in Glacier National Park.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanna198895–111-279]

### Major cultural events



Dancers at Crow Fair in 1941

Montana hosts numerous arts and cultural festivals and events every year. Major events include:

- [Bozeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bozeman,_Montana) was once known as the "Sweet Pea capital of the nation" referencing the prolific edible pea crop. To promote the area and celebrate its prosperity, local business owners began a "Sweet Pea Carnival" that included a parade and queen contest. The annual event lasted from 1906 to 1916. Promoters used the inedible but fragrant and colorful [sweet pea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_pea) flower as an emblem of the celebration. In 1977 the "Sweet Pea" concept was revived as an arts festival rather than a harvest celebration, growing into a three-day event that is one of the largest festivals in Montana.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurlbutDavis2009179–181-280]

- Montana Shakespeare in the Parks has been performing free, live theatrical productions of Shakespeare and other classics throughout Montana and the Northwest region since 1973. The organization is an outreach endeavor that is part of the College of Arts & Architecture at Montana State University, Bozeman.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShakespeare_in_the_Parks2012-281]

  &#x20;The Montana Shakespeare Company is based in Helena.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTELincoln2012-282]

- Since 1909, the [Crow Fair and Rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crow_Fair), near [Hardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardin,_Montana), has been an annual event every August in [Crow Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crow_Agency,_Montana) and is the largest Northern Native American gathering, attracting nearly 45,000 spectators and participants.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScandale2011-283]

  &#x20;Since 1952, North American Indian Days has been held every July in Browning.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTENorth_American_Indian_Days2013-284]

- [Lame Deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lame_Deer,_Montana) hosts the annual Northern Cheyenne Powwow.

### Sports



[Montana State Bobcats football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_State_Bobcats_football) at [Bobcat Stadium (Montana State University)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobcat_Stadium_\(Montana_State_University\)), Bozeman

#### Professional sports

There are no [major league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada) sports franchises in Montana due to the state's relatively small and dispersed population, but a number of [minor league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_league) teams play in the state. [Baseball](3850.md) is the minor-league sport with the longest heritage in the state and Montana is home to four [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_baseball_league) teams, all members of the [Pioneer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pioneer_League_\(baseball\)): the [Billings Mustangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billings_Mustangs), [Great Falls Voyagers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Falls_Voyagers), [Missoula PaddleHeads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missoula_PaddleHeads) and the [Glacier Range Riders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier_Range_Riders).

#### College sports

All of Montana's four-year colleges and universities field intercollegiate sports teams. The two largest schools, the [University of Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Montana-Missoula) and [Montana State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_State_University_-_Bozeman), are members of the [Big Sky Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sky_Conference) and have enjoyed a strong athletic rivalry since the early twentieth century. Six of Montana's smaller four-year schools are members of the [Frontier Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontier_Conference).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrotier_Conference2013-285] One is a member of the [Great Northwest Athletic Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northwest_Athletic_Conference).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreat_Northwest_Athletic_Conference2013-286]

#### Other sports

A variety of sports are offered at Montana high schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_High_School_Association2013-287] Montana allows the smallest—"Class C"—high schools to utilize [six-man football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six-man_football) teams,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_High_School_Association-Football2013139–141-288] dramatized in the independent 2002 film *[The Slaughter Rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Slaughter_Rule)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolden2002-289]

There are four junior [ice hockey](14790.md) teams in Montana, all of which are affiliated with the [North American 3 Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_3_Hockey_League): the [Bozeman Icedogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bozeman_Icedogs), Butte Irish, [Great Falls Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Falls_Americans), and [Helena Bighorns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena_Bighorns).

#### Olympic competitors

- [Ski jumping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ski_jumping) champion and [United States Skiing Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ski_Hall_of_Fame) inductee [Casper Oimoen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casper_Oimoen?action=edit\&redlink=1) was captain of the U.S. Olympic team at the [1936 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_Winter_Olympics) while he was a resident of Montana. He placed thirteenth that year, and had previously finished fifth at the [1932 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1932_Winter_Olympics).

  [^cite_note-Dawson-290]

  [^cite_note-Eriksmoen-291]

- Montana has produced two [U.S. champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Figure_Skating_Championships) and Olympic competitors in [men's figure skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men's_figure_skating), both from Great Falls: [John Misha Petkevich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Misha_Petkevich), lived and trained in Montana before entering college, competed in the 1968 and 1972 [Winter Olympics](34069.md).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESports_Reference2013-292]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOttum1968-293]

  &#x20;[Scott Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Davis_\(figure_skater\)), also from Great Falls, competed at the [1994 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Winter_Olympics).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIce_Network2012-294]

- Missoulian [Tommy Moe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Moe) won Olympic gold and silver medals at the [1994 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Winter_Olympics) in [downhill skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downhill_skiing) and [super G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_G), the first American skier to win two medals at any Winter Olympics.

  [^cite_note-Moe-295]

- [Eric Bergoust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Bergoust), also of Missoula, won an Olympic gold medal in [freestyle aerial skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freestyle_skiing#Aerial_skiing) at the [1998 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Winter_Olympics), also competing in 1994, 2002 and 2006 Olympics plus winning 13 World Cup titles.

  [^cite_note-Bergoust-296]

- [Maggie Voisin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maggie_Voisin), of Whitefish, competed in the 2018 and 2022 Winter Olympics as a skier. She also qualified for the 2014 Winter Olympics but was unable to compete as she broke her ankle during training.

  [^cite_note-297]

#### Sporting achievements

Montanans have been a part of several major sporting achievements:

- In 1889, [Spokane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spokane_\(horse\)) became the first and only Montana horse to win the [Kentucky Derby](17135.md). For this accomplishment, the horse was admitted to the [Montana Cowboy Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Cowboy_Hall_of_Fame) in 2008.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKentucky_Derby2013-298]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Cowboy_Hall_of_Fame2013-299]

- In 1904 a [basketball](3921.md) team of young Native American women from [Fort Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Shaw,_Montana), after playing undefeated during their previous season, went to the [Louisiana Purchase Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase_Exposition) held in St. Louis in 1904, defeated all challenging teams and were declared to be world champions.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeaveySmith2008-300]

- In 1923, the controversial [Jack Dempsey vs. Tommy Gibbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Dempsey_vs._Tommy_Gibbons) fight for the heavyweight boxing championship, won by Dempsey, took place in [Shelby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby,_Montana).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson1966-301]



Lone Mountain at [Big Sky Ski Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sky_Ski_Resort)

### Outdoor recreation

Montana provides year-round outdoor recreation opportunities for residents and visitors. Hiking, fishing, hunting, watercraft recreation, camping, golf, cycling, horseback riding, and skiing are popular activities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Outdoor_Recreation2013-302]

#### Fishing and hunting

Montana has been a destination for its world-class trout fisheries since the 1930s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchullery2006a31–50-303] [Fly fishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly_fishing) for several species of native and introduced [trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trout) in rivers and lakes is popular for both residents and tourists throughout the state. Montana is the home of the [Federation of Fly Fishers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Fly_Fishers) and hosts many of the organization's annual conclaves. The state has robust recreational [lake trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_trout) and [kokanee salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kokanee_salmon) fisheries in the west, [walleye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walleye) can be found in many parts of the state, while [northern pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_pike), [smallmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallmouth_bass) and [largemouth bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largemouth_bass) fisheries as well as [catfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catfish) and [paddlefish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddlefish) can be found in the waters of eastern Montana.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisheries_Management_Plan2013-304] [Robert Redford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Redford)'s [1992 film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_River_Runs_Through_It_\(film\)) of [Norman Mclean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Mclean)'s novel, *[A River Runs Through It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_River_Runs_Through_It_\(novel\))*, was filmed in Montana and brought national attention to fly fishing and the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchullery2006b167–186-305] Fishing makes up a sizeable component of Montana's total tourism economic output: in 2017, nonresidents generated \$4.7 billion in economic output, of which, \$1.3 billion was generated by visitor groups participating in guided fishing experiences.[^cite_note-306]

There are fall bow and general hunting seasons for [elk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elk), [pronghorn antelope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pronghorn), [whitetail deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitetail_deer) and [mule deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mule_deer). A random draw grants a limited number of permits for [moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose), [mountain goats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_goat) and [bighorn sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bighorn_sheep). There is a spring hunting season for [black bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_black_bear) and limited hunting of [bison that leave Yellowstone National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowstone_Park_bison_herd) has been allowed. Current law allows both hunters and trappers specified numbers ("limits") of [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf) and [mountain lions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_lion). Trapping of assorted fur-bearing animals is allowed in certain seasons and many opportunities exist for migratory waterfowl and upland bird hunting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Hunting_Regulations2012-307][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Trapping2013-308] The [Rocky Mountain Elk Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountain_Elk_Foundation), which protects wildlife habitat and promotes hunting heritage, was founded in Montana.

#### Winter sports



The [Big Sky Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sky_Resort)



The Palisades area on the north end of the ski area at [Red Lodge Mountain Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lodge_Mountain_Resort)



Guided snowmobile tours in Yellowstone Park

Both [downhill skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downhill_skiing) and [cross-country skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-country_skiing) are popular in Montana, and there are 15 developed downhill ski areas open to the public,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinter_Montana—Resorts2010-309] including:

- [Bear Paw Ski Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bear_Paw_Ski_Bowl) near [Havre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havre,_Montana)

- [Big Sky Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sky_Resort) in [Big Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sky,_Montana)

- [Blacktail Mountain Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blacktail_Mountain_Ski_Area) near [Lakeside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeside,_Montana)

- [Bridger Bowl Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridger_Bowl_Ski_Area) near [Bozeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bozeman,_Montana)

- [Discovery Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_Basin) near [Philipsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philipsburg,_Montana)

- [Great Divide Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Divide_Ski_Area) near [Helena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena,_Montana)

- [Lookout Pass Ski and Recreation Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lookout_Pass_Ski_and_Recreation_Area) off [Interstate 90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_90) at the Montana-Idaho border

- [Lost Trail Powder Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lost_Trail_Powder_Mountain) near [Darby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darby,_Montana)

- [Maverick Mountain Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maverick_Mountain_Ski_Area) near [Dillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dillon,_Montana)

- [Montana Snowbowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Snowbowl) near [Missoula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missoula,_Montana)

- [Red Lodge Mountain Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lodge_Mountain_Resort) near [Red Lodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lodge,_Montana)

- [Showdown Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Showdown_Ski_Area) near [White Sulphur Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Sulphur_Springs,_Montana)

- [Teton Pass Ski Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teton_Pass_Ski_Area) near [Choteau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choteau,_Montana)

- [Turner Mountain Ski Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turner_Mountain_Ski_Resort) near [Libby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libby,_Montana)

- [Whitefish Mountain Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitefish_Mountain_Resort) near [Whitefish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitefish,_Montana)

Big Sky Resort and Whitefish Mountain Resort are [destination resorts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conference_and_resort_hotels), while the remaining areas do not have overnight lodging at the ski area, though several host restaurants and other amenities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinter_Montana—Resorts2010-309]

Montana also has millions of acres open to cross-country skiing on nine of its national forests and in Glacier National Park. In addition to cross-country trails at most of the downhill ski areas, there are also 13 private cross-country skiing resorts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinter_Montana—XC2010-310] Yellowstone National Park also allows cross-country skiing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Park_Service-Yellowstone2013-311]

[Snowmobiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowmobile) is popular in Montana, which boasts over 4,000 miles of trails and frozen lakes available in winter.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Snowmobiling2013-312] There are 24 areas where snowmobile trails are maintained, most also offering ungroomed trails.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinter_Montana-Snowmobiling2010-313] [West Yellowstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Yellowstone,_Montana) offers a large selection of trails and is the primary starting point for snowmobile trips into Yellowstone National Park,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinter_Montana-West_Yellowstone2010-314] where "oversnow" vehicle use is strictly limited, usually to guided tours, and regulations are in considerable flux.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Park_Service-Yellowstone_in_Winter2013-315]

[Snow coach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_coach) tours are offered at Big Sky, Whitefish, West Yellowstone and into Yellowstone National Park.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinter_Montana-Snowcoaches2010-316] Equestrian [skijoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skijoring) has a niche in Montana, which hosts the World Skijoring Championships in Whitefish as part of the annual [Whitefish Winter Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitefish_Winter_Carnival?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhitefish_Skijoring2013-317]

## Health

Montana has two [Trauma I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Level_I_trauma_center) hospitals, both in Billings. The Billings Clinic hospital became the first in Montana and neighboring Wyoming.[^cite_note-318] In 2024, Intermountain Health's St. Vincent Regional Hospital also received verification.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] There are [Trauma II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Level_II_trauma_center) hospitals in Missoula and Great Falls.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrauma_Centers2013-321] In 2013, *[AARP The Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AARP_The_Magazine)* named the [Billings Clinic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billings_Clinic) one of the safest hospitals in the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowardB201346–50-322] In 2023, Billings Clinic united with Kalispell-based [Logan Health Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logan_Health_Medical_Center) to form the largest Montana-based health system in the state, with coverage in all corners of the state and into Wyoming.[^cite_note-Billings_Clinic_and_Logan_Health_Unite-323]

Montana is ranked as the least obese state in the U.S., at 19.6%, according to the 2014 Gallup Poll.[^cite_note-Gallup_Obesity_Poll-324]

Montana had a [suicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_rate) of 26.6 per 100,000 in 2023, which is the second highest of all the states in the U.S., only falling behind [Alaska](624.md).[^cite_note-325] High suicide rates are common among sparsely populated states in the United States.[^cite_note-cdcstats-326][^cite_note-cdcsuicide-327]

## Media

Main articles: [List of newspapers in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Montana), [List of radio stations in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Montana), and [List of television stations in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_stations_in_Montana)

As of 2010, Missoula is the 166th largest [media market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_market) in the United States as ranked by [Nielsen Media Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_Media_Research), while Billings is 170th, Great Falls is 190th, the Butte/Bozeman area is 191st, and Helena is 206th.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENielsen2010-328] There are 25 [television stations in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_stations_in_Montana), representing each major [U.S. network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_over-the-air_television_networks).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStation_Index2013-329] As of August 2013, there are 527 [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission)-licensed FM [radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio) [stations broadcast in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Montana), with 114 such AM stations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFCC-FM2013-330][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFCC-AM2013-331]

During the age of the [Copper Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_Kings), each Montana copper company had its own newspaper. This changed in 1959 when [Lee Enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Enterprises) bought several Montana newspapers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee_Enterprises-Newspapers2013-332][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee_Enterprises-History2013-333] Montana's largest circulating daily city newspapers are the *[Billings Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billings_Gazette)* (circulation 39,405), *[Great Falls Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Falls_Tribune)* (26,733), and *[Missoulian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missoulian)* (25,439).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMondo_Times2013-334]

In May 2023, Montana became the first US state to ban the social media app [TikTok](56822861.md) and online marketplace [Temu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temu_\(company\)).[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]

## Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Montana)

See also: [List of Montana railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Montana_railroads), [List of Montana numbered highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Montana_numbered_highways), and [List of airports in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Montana)



[Yellowstone Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowstone_Airport), [West Yellowstone, Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Yellowstone,_Montana)

Railroads have been an important method of transportation in Montana since the 1880s. Historically, the state was traversed by the main lines of three east–west transcontinental routes: the [Milwaukee Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Road), the [Great Northern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northern_Railway_\(U.S.\)), and the [Northern Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Pacific_Railway). Today, the [BNSF Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF_Railway) is the state's largest railroad, its main transcontinental route incorporating the former Great Northern main line across the state. [Montana RailLink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_RailLink), a privately held [Class II railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_II_railroad), operated former Northern Pacific trackage in western Montana before being bought out by BNSF.

In addition, [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak)'s *[Empire Builder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_Builder)* train runs through the north of the state, stopping in [Libby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libby,_Montana), [Whitefish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitefish,_Montana), [West Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Glacier,_Montana), [Essex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essex,_Montana), [East Glacier Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Glacier_Park_Village,_Montana), [Browning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Browning,_Montana), [Cut Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cut_Bank,_Montana), [Shelby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby,_Montana), [Havre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havre,_Montana), [Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malta,_Montana), [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow,_Montana), and [Wolf Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_Point,_Montana).

Intercity bus service in Montana is provided by [Jefferson Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Lines) and Express Arrow.

[Bozeman Yellowstone International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bozeman_Yellowstone_International_Airport) is the busiest airport in the state of Montana, surpassing [Billings Logan International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billings_Logan_International_Airport) in the spring of 2013.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBacaj2013-338][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKTVQ2013-339] Montana's other major airports include [Missoula International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missoula_International_Airport), [Great Falls International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Falls_International_Airport), [Glacier Park International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier_Park_International_Airport), [Helena Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena_Regional_Airport), [Bert Mooney Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bert_Mooney_Airport), and [Yellowstone Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowstone_Airport). Eight smaller communities have airports designated for commercial service under the [Essential Air Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_Air_Service) program.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartment_of_Transportation2012-340]



[U.S. Route 93](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_93_in_Montana) in Montana

Historically, [U.S. Route 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_10) was the primary east–west highway route across Montana, connecting the major cities in the southern half of the state. Still, the state's most important east–west travel corridor, the route is today served by [Interstate 90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_90) and [Interstate 94](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_94) which roughly follow the same route as the Northern Pacific. [U.S. Routes 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_2) and [12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_12) and [Montana Highway 200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Highway_200) also traverse the entire state from east to west.

Montana's only north–south [Interstate Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highway) is [Interstate 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_15_in_Montana). Other major north–south highways include [U.S. Routes 87](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_87), [89](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_89), [93](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_93), and [191](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_191).

Montana and [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota) are the only states to share a land border that is not traversed by a paved road. Highway 212, the primary paved route between the two, passes through the northeast corner of Wyoming between Montana and South Dakota.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESites2012-341][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Transportation2013-342]

## Law and government

Main article: [Montana State Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_State_Government)

### Constitution

Montana is governed by a constitution. The first constitution was drafted by a constitutional convention in 1889, in preparation for statehood. Ninety percent of its language came from an 1884 constitution which was never acted upon by Congress for national political reasons. The 1889 constitution mimicked the structure of the [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution), as well as outlining almost the same civil and political rights for citizens. However, the 1889 Montana constitution significantly restricted the power of state government, the legislature was much more powerful than the executive branch, and the jurisdiction of the [District Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_District_Courts) very specifically described.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElisonSnyder20013–4,_144-343] Montana voters amended the 1889 constitution 37 times between 1889 and 1972.[^cite_note-344] In 1914, Montana [granted women the vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage). In 1916, Montana became the first state to elect a woman, [Progressive Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) [Jeannette Rankin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeannette_Rankin), to Congress.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWomen's_National_History2013-345][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJeannette_Rankin2011-346]

In 1971, Montana voters approved the call for a state constitutional convention. A new constitution was drafted, which made the legislative and executive branches much more equal in power and which was much less prescriptive in outlining powers, duties, and jurisdictions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElisonSnyder2001144-347] The draft included an expanded, more [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism) list of civil and political rights, extended these rights to children for the first time, transferred administration of property taxes to the counties from the state, implemented new [water rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_rights), eliminated [sovereign immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_immunity), and gave the legislature greater power to spend tax revenues. The constitution was narrowly approved, 116,415 to 113,883, and declared ratified on June 20, 1972. Three issues that the constitutional convention was unable to resolve were submitted to voters simultaneously with the proposed constitution. Voters approved the legalization of gambling, a bicameral legislature, and retention of the death penalty.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElisonSnyder200116–18-348]

The 1972 constitution has been amended 31 times as of 2015.[^cite_note-sos1972current-349] Major amendments include establishment of a [reclamation trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reclamation) (funded by taxes on natural resource extraction) to restore mined land (1974); restoration of sovereign immunity, when such immunity has been approved by a two-thirds vote in each house (1974); establishment of a 90-day biennial (rather than annual) legislative session (1974); establishment of a coal tax trust fund, funded by a tax on coal extraction (1976); conversion of the mandatory decennial review of county government into a voluntary one, to be approved or disallowed by residents in each county (1978); conversion of the provision of public assistance from a mandatory civil right to a non-fundamental legislative prerogative (1988);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElisonSnyder200119–20-350] a new constitutional right to hunt and fish (2004); a now-defunct prohibition on [same-sex marriage](92656.md) (2004); a prohibition on new taxes on the sale or transfer of real property (2010), and a constitutional provision codifying [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Montana) rights (2024).[^cite_note-sos1972current-349][^cite_note-351] In 1992, voters approved a constitutional amendment implementing [term limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_limits) for certain statewide elected executive branch offices (governor, lieutenant governor, secretary of state, state auditor, attorney general, superintendent of public instruction) and for members of the [Montana Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Legislature). Extensive new constitutional rights for victims of crime were approved in 2016.[^cite_note-352]

The 1972 constitution requires that voters determine every 20 years whether to hold a new constitutional convention. Voters turned down a new convention in 1990 (84 percent no)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElisonSnyder200119-353] and again in 2010 (58.6 percent no).[^cite_note-354]

### Executive

See also: [List of governors of Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Montana)

Montana has three branches of state government: legislative, executive, and judicial. The executive branch is headed by an elected governor. The governor is [Greg Gianforte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Gianforte), a Republican elected in 2020. There are also nine other statewide elected offices in the executive branch: Lieutenant Governor, Attorney General, Secretary of State, State Auditor (who also serves as Commissioner of Securities and Insurance), and Superintendent of Public Instruction. There are five public service commissioners, who are elected on a regional basis. (The Public Service Commission's jurisdiction is statewide.)

There are 18 departments and offices which make up the executive branch: Administration; Agriculture; Auditor (securities and insurance); Commerce; Corrections; Environmental Quality; Fish, Wildlife & Parks; Justice; Labor and Industry; Livestock; Military Affairs; Natural Resources and Conservation; Public Health and Human Services; Revenue; State; and Transportation. Elementary and secondary education are overseen by the Office of Public Instruction (led by the elected superintendent of public instruction), in cooperation with the governor-appointed Board of Public Education. Higher education is overseen by a governor-appointed Board of Regents, which in turn appoints a commissioner of higher education. The Office of the Commissioner of Higher Education acts in an executive capacity on behalf of the regents and oversees the state-run [Montana University System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_University_System).

Independent state agencies not within a department or office include the Montana Arts Council, Montana Board of Crime Control, Montana Historical Society, Montana Public Employees Retirement Administration, Commissioner of Political Practices, the Montana Lottery, Office of the State Public Defender, Public Service Commission, the Montana School for the Deaf and Blind, the Montana State Fund (which operates the state's unemployment insurance, worker compensation, and self-insurance operations), the Montana State Library, and the Montana Teachers Retirement System.

Montana is an [alcoholic beverage control state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholic_beverage_control_state).[^cite_note-355] It is an [equitable distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_property) and [no-fault divorce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-fault_divorce) state. It is one of five states to have no [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax).[^cite_note-356]

### Legislative

The [Montana Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Legislature) is bicameral and consists of the 50-member [Montana Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Senate) and the 100-member [Montana House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_House_of_Representatives). The legislature meets in the [Montana State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_State_Capitol) in Helena in odd-numbered years for 90 days, beginning the first weekday of the year. The deadline for a legislator to introduce a general bill is the 40th legislative day. The deadline for a legislator to introduce an appropriations, revenue, or referendum bill is the 62nd legislative day. Senators serve four-year terms, while Representatives serve two-year terms. All members are limited to serving no more than eight years in a single 16-year period.

### Judicial

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Montana) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(December 2025)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

The [Courts of Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courts_of_Montana) are established by the Constitution of Montana. The constitution requires the establishment of a [Montana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Supreme_Court) and [Montana District Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_District_Courts), and permits the legislature to establish [Justice Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_inferior_courts), City Courts, Municipal Courts, and other inferior courts such as the legislature sees fit to establish.

The Montana Supreme Court is the court of last resort in the Montana court system. The constitution of 1889 provided for the election of no fewer than three Supreme Court justices, and one chief justice. Each court member served a six-year term. The legislature increased the number of justices to five in 1919. The 1972 constitution lengthened the term of office to eight years and established the minimum number of justices at five. It allowed the legislature to increase the number of justices by two, which the legislature did in 1979. The Montana Supreme Court has the authority to declare acts of the legislature and executive unconstitutional under either the Montana or U.S. constitutions. Its decisions may be appealed directly to the U.S. Supreme Court. The [Clerk of the Montana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerk_of_the_Montana_Supreme_Court) is also an elected position and serves a six-year term.[^cite_note-357] Neither justices nor the clerk is term-limited.

Montana District Courts are the courts of general jurisdiction in Montana. There are no intermediate appellate courts. District Courts have jurisdiction primarily over most civil cases, cases involving a monetary claim against the state, felony criminal cases, probate, and cases at law and in equity. When so authorized by the legislature, actions of executive branch agencies may be appealed directly to a District Court. The District Courts also have *de novo* appellate jurisdiction from inferior courts (city courts, justice courts, and municipal courts), and oversee naturalization proceedings. District Court judges are elected and serve six-year terms. They are not term-limited. There are 22 judicial districts in Montana, served by 56 District Courts and 46 District Court judges. The District Courts suffer from excessive workload, and the legislature has struggled to find a solution to the problem.

[Montana Youth Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Youth_Courts) were established by the Montana Youth Court Act of 1974. They are overseen by District Court judges. They consist of a chief probation officer, one or more juvenile probation officers, and support staff. Youth Courts have jurisdiction over misdemeanor and felony acts committed by those charged as a juvenile under the law. There is a Youth Court in every judicial district, and decisions of the Youth Court are appealable directly to the Montana Supreme Court.

The Montana Worker's Compensation Court was established by the Montana Workers' Compensation Act in 1975.[^cite_note-358] There is a single Workers' Compensation Court. It has a single judge, appointed by the governor. The Worker's Compensation Court has statewide jurisdiction and holds trials in Billings, Great Falls, Helena, Kalispell, and Missoula. The court hears cases arising under the Montana Workers' Compensation Act and is the court of original jurisdiction for reviews of orders and regulations issued by the Montana Department of Labor and Industry. Decisions of the court are appealable directly to the Montana Supreme Court.

The [Montana Water Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Water_Court) was established by the Montana Water Court Act of 1979. The Water Court consists of a chief water judge and four district water judges (Lower Missouri River Basin, Upper Missouri River Basin, Yellowstone River Basin, and Clark Fork River Basin). The court employs 12 permanent special masters. The Montana Judicial Nomination Commission develops short lists of nominees for all five Water Judges, who are then appointed by the Chief justice of the Montana Supreme Court (subject to confirmation by the Montana Senate). The Water Court adjudicates water rights claims under the Montana Water Use Act of 1973 and has statewide jurisdiction.[^cite_note-359] District Courts have the authority to enforce decisions of the Water Court, but only the Montana Supreme Court has the authority to review decisions of the Water Court.

From 1889 to 1909, elections for judicial office in Montana were partisan. Beginning in 1909, these elections became nonpartisan. The Montana Supreme Court struck down the nonpartisan law in 1911 on technical grounds, but a new law was enacted in 1935 which barred political parties from endorsing, making contributions to, or making expenditures on behalf of or against judicial candidates. In 2012, the U.S. Supreme Court struck down Montana's judicial nonpartisan election law in *[American Tradition Partnership, Inc. v. Bullock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Tradition_Partnership,_Inc._v._Bullock)*, 567 U.S. \_\_\_\_ (Sup.Ct. 2012).Although candidates must remain nonpartisan, spending by partisan entities is now permitted. Spending on state supreme court races exponentially increased to \$1.6 million in 2014, and to more than \$1.6 million in 2016 (both new records).

### Federal offices and courts

See also: [Montana's congressional delegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana's_congressional_delegations)

The U.S. Constitution provides each state with two senators. Montana's two U.S. senators are [Steve Daines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Daines), first elected in 2014 and later reelected in 2020, and [Tim Sheehy](77920271.md), elected in 2024. Both are Republicans. The U.S. Constitution provides each state with a single representative, with additional representatives apportioned based on population. From statehood in 1889 until 1913, Montana was represented in the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md) by a single representative, elected at-large. Montana received a second representative in 1913, following the 1910 census and reapportionment. Both members, however, were still elected at-large. Beginning in 1919, Montana moved to district, rather than at-large, elections for its two House members. This created [Montana's 1st congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana's_1st_congressional_district) in the west and [Montana's 2nd congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana's_2nd_congressional_district) in the east.

Montana had only one representative in the U.S. House after having lost its second district in the 1990 census reapportionment. The remaining seat was again elected [at-large](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/At-large). Montana's population grew at about the national average during the 2000s, but it had failed to regain its second seat in 2010.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECongressional_Delegation2013-360] In the reapportionment following the 2020 census, Montana regained a House seat, increasing the state's number of representatives in the House to two after a thirty-year break, starting from 2023.[^cite_note-361] Before the 2020 reapportionment, Montana's at-large congressional district held the largest population of any district in the country.[^cite_note-362]

Republicans [Troy Downing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy_Downing) and [Ryan Zinke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_Zinke) are the current officeholders.

Montana's Senate district is the fourth largest by area, behind Alaska, Texas, and California. The most notorious of Montana's early senators was [William A. Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_A._Clark), a "[Copper King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_Kings)" and one of the 50 richest Americans ever. He is well known for having bribed his way into the U.S. Senate. Among Montana's most historically prominent senators are [Thomas J. Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_J._Walsh) (serving from 1913 to 1933), who was President-elect Franklin D. Roosevelt's choice for attorney general when he died; [Burton K. Wheeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burton_K._Wheeler) (serving from 1923 to 1947), an oft-mentioned presidential candidate and strong supporter of isolationism; [Mike Mansfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Mansfield), the longest-serving [Senate majority leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_leaders_of_the_United_States_Senate) in U.S. history; [Max Baucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Baucus) (served 1978 to 2014), longest-serving U.S. senator in Montana history, and the senator who shepherded the [Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act) through the Senate in 2010; and [Lee Metcalf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Metcalf) (served 1961 to 1978), a pioneer of the environmental movement.

Montana's House district is the largest congressional district in the United States by population, with just over 1,023,000 constituents. It is the second-largest House district by area, after Alaska's at-large congressional district. Of Montana's House delegates, [Jeannette Rankin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeannette_Rankin) was the first woman to hold national office in the United States when she was elected to the U.S. House of Representatives in 1916.[^cite_note-363] Also notable is Representative (later Senator) [Thomas H. Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_H._Carter), the first Catholic to serve as chairman of the [Republican National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Committee) (from 1892 to 1896).[^cite_note-364]

Federal courts in Montana include the [United States District Court for the District of Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_District_of_Montana) and the United States Bankruptcy Court for the District of Montana. Three former Montana politicians have been named judges on the U.S. District Court: [Charles Nelson Pray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Nelson_Pray) (who served in the U.S. House of Representatives from 1907 to 1913), [James F. Battin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_F._Battin) (who served in the U.S. House of Representatives from 1961 to 1969), and [Paul G. Hatfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_G._Hatfield) (who served as an appointed U.S. Senator in 1978). [Brian Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Morris_\(judge\)), who served as an associate justice of the Montana Supreme Court from 2005 to 2013, currently serves as a judge on the court.

## Politics

Further information: [Political party strength in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_Montana) and [Elections in Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Montana)

Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Montana#). [You can help](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Montana\&action=edit). The talk page may contain suggestions. *(May 2025)* |
| --- | --- |



[Treemap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treemap) of the popular vote by county in the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election_in_Montana). Areas are in proportion to the number of votes cast in each county.

Elections in the state had been historically competitive, particularly for state-level offices. Historically, the [Democratic Party](5043544.md)'s strength in the state was gained from support among unionized miners and railroad workers, while farmers generally voted Republican. Republicans have been strongest in the east, while Democrats have been strongest in the west.

Montana had a history of voters [splitting their tickets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split-ticket_voting) and filling elected offices with individuals from both parties. Through the mid-20th century, the state had a tradition of "sending the liberals to Washington and the conservatives to Helena". Between 1988 and 2006, the pattern flipped, with voters more likely to elect conservatives to federal offices. There have also been long-term shifts in party control. From 1968 through 1988, the state was dominated by the Democratic Party, with Democratic governors for a 20-year period, and a Democratic majority of both the national congressional delegation and during many sessions of the state legislature. This pattern shifted, beginning with the 1988 election when Montana elected a [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Republican_Party) governor for the first time since 1964 and sent a Republican to the U.S. Senate for the first time since 1948. This shift continued with the [reapportionment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reapportionment) of the state's legislative districts that took effect in 1994, when the Republican Party took control of both chambers of the state legislature, consolidating a Republican party dominance that lasted until the 2004 reapportionment produced more swing districts and a brief period of Democratic legislative majorities in the mid-2000s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_Legislature2012-365]

At the federal level, the pattern of split-ticket voting and divided government endured until Democratic [U.S. Senator](24909346.md) [Jon Tester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Tester) [lost reelection in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_Senate_election_in_Montana), subsequently ceding his seat on January 3, 2025, thereby leaving Montana with no Democratic statewide officeholders.[^cite_note-366]

Montana has voted for the Republican nominee in all but two presidential elections since 1952.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontana_2012_Election2013-370] The state last supported a Democrat for president in 1992, when [Bill Clinton](3356.md) won a plurality victory. The state is somewhat less Republican than its neighboring states (Idaho, Wyoming, South Dakota, and North Dakota), all of which last supported a Democratic presidential candidate in [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election).

In the [2008 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election), Montana was considered a [swing state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) and was ultimately won by Republican [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) by a narrow margin of two percent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2008_Election2008-371] The two chambers of the state's legislature had split party control from 2004 to 2010, when that year's mid-term elections decisively returned both branches to Republican control.

The state's congressional seats (now two districts, but until 2023, one [at-large district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana's_at-large_congressional_district)) have been Republican since 1996, and its Senate seats have been held by Republicans [Steve Daines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Daines) since 2015 and [Tim Sheehy](77920271.md) since 2025.

The [Montana Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Senate) is controlled by Republicans 32 to 18, and the [Montana House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_House_of_Representatives) also holds a 57 to 43 Republican majority following the [2024 Montana elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Montana_elections) which saw Democrats break the Republican supermajority in the House aided by [independent redistricting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_redistricting_commission).

In a 2020 study, Montana was ranked as the 21st easiest state for citizens to vote in.[^cite_note-372]
